[2:22:24] Those audit standards, generally accepted government auditing standards, or GAGAS— you can see here
[2:22:30] it's in a yellow book— provide a framework for users to rely upon the

[2:23:24] deeply entrenched in our firm. They're literally wired into our DNA.
[2:23:30] While performing performance audits provides objective analysis to assist management and improve performance, A
[2:23:38] critical principle of GAGAS audits is to serve the public interest.
[2:23:43] As part of that service, audit standards set forth requirements in 3 areas: general,
[2:23:48] fieldwork, and reporting.
[2:23:51] The general standards speak to characteristics that auditors must possess to provide credibility of
[2:23:57] our work, such as independence, professional judgment, and competency.
[2:24:02] Fieldwork standards prescribe the approach to ensure audits are properly planned and supervised, and
[2:24:07] that results are based on documented evidence that is sufficient and appropriate.
[2:24:14] Finally, the reporting standards contain several requirements, including obtaining the views of responsible officials.
[2:24:21] So guiding the work of GAGAS auditors are these ethical principles of integrity, objectivity,
[2:24:25] and professional behavior. As such, we must perform these responsibilities employing an attitude that
[2:24:31] is unbiased, fact-based, nonpartisan, and nonideological regarding the audited entity.
[2:24:39] This includes an independence of mind and appearance and being free of conflicts.
[2:24:45] Maintaining that independence and objectivity requires, requires a continual assessment of relationships with audited
[2:24:51] entities in the context of our responsibility to the public.
[2:24:56] For our firm, we assess independence on an annual basis.
[2:25:00] and each time we begin an audit engagement.
[2:25:03] We apply the GAGAS conceptual frameworks, make independence determinations based on the circumstances of
[2:25:09] each audit, and assure that we remain independent throughout the audit.
[2:25:14] In addition to the GAGAS ethics and independence requirements, we must also follow the
[2:25:19] codes of conduct of our professional organizations that our team belongs to, the AICPA,
[2:25:25] IIA, and NACA. We have no stake in the audit results and are an
[2:25:30] independent voice in the room, as it should be.
[2:25:33] We aren't responsible for implementing the bond, managing the bond, overseeing the bond, or
[2:25:37] advising on the bond. Rather, we are an independent, neutral party for the public
[2:25:44] to ensure we remain independent and have evidence to support findings.
[2:25:49] Our underlying bond audit work and report have undergone extensive quality control processes to
[2:25:55] provide assurance that we've fulfilled our responsibilities under standards and verify that we have
[2:26:01] audit evidence to support our conclusions. As required by GAGAS, our work is subject
[2:26:07] to a detailed external peer review conducted by professional GAGAS auditors, so we get
[2:26:13] audited too, every 3 years to determine that our work complies with audit standards.
[2:26:19] And our firm has continually received clean opinions that our work complies.
[2:26:25] As part of our independence, GAAGIS requires we exercise professional skepticism in the conduct
[2:26:30] of our work, meaning we maintain a questioning mind, stay aware of possible misstatements
[2:26:36] that could be indicative of an error or fraud, and conduct critical assessments of
[2:26:40] evidence. While we listen to auditees and stakeholders we interview, often that have disparate
[2:26:47] perspectives on a topic or a process. We corroborate or refute information we received
[2:26:52] against audit evidence independently gathered or developed from analysis as our part of review—
[2:26:58] of our review of controls and risks. Standards prescribe that auditors must use their
[2:27:04] professional judgment, much like that of a certified architect or an engineer, when applying
[2:27:10] their body of knowledge building one of your schools.
[2:27:13] That audit judgment represents our collective knowledge, skills, and abilities, in addition to hours
[2:27:17] of continuing education we must obtain to critically assess and determine the sufficiency and
[2:27:24] appropriateness of evidence.
[2:27:29] For this bond audit cycle, setting the audit scope has been the same process
[2:27:33] each year. It starts with possible scope areas as defined in our contract which
[2:27:38] really cover all areas of capital delivery from planning to contracting and design into
[2:27:44] construction and closeout as well as oversight.
[2:27:49] Using those areas as a backdrop, we meet with management and staff, board members,
[2:27:54] BAC members, and other stakeholders to get input on areas of concern or challenge.
[2:28:00] We combine that input with our own consideration of other factors Such as maybe
[2:28:05] looking at an area that has not been previously audited, known changes to a
[2:28:09] program, or even industry threats that might identify potential areas to audit.
[2:28:16] We also consider phasing. Would it make sense to audit construction management if something
[2:28:21] is just still in the design phase? So based on that planning effort, we
[2:28:26] draft an initial work plan with estimated tasks and budget to discuss with the
[2:28:30] prior OSM, for approval of general areas. However, after that initial scope is established,
[2:28:37] we still might review other areas depending on the risk assessment we conduct.

[2:28:47] to our attention throughout the audit cycle. Thus, audit planning continues throughout the audit.

[2:30:59] or misleading information, lack of key documentation, unusual patterns in contracting, and management's willingness

[2:52:49] board policy and statutes that give the board much broader authority over district activities,
[2:52:55] property, personnel, contracts, and finances. Other school board associations also view boards as being
[2:53:02] responsible for overseeing those types of financial decisions and ensuring resources are being used
[2:53:08] efficiently. Because it can be difficult to distinguish oversight responsibilities of the board from
[2:53:15] the superintendent's management of the district, We made recommendations for the board to establish

[2:53:26] to monitor bond activities and be accountable. Further, we found the board could more
[2:53:32] strongly embrace the role of internal audits. In fact, the internal auditor plays a
[2:53:37] big role in fulfilling the board's responsibilities as part of the district's control environment.
[2:53:43] Both the IIA and COSO speak to internal audits as an independent extension of
[2:53:48] board oversight with respect to how well internal controls are functioning, in addition to
[2:53:54] assessing whether key risks are being managed and whether information presented to the board
